ngAfterContentChecked() runs after every change detection cycle for the projected content (ng-content). Used for debugging, or tracking changes in projected content.

Lifecycle hook

ngAfterViewInit()

ngAfterViewInit() runs once, after the component's view and child views are fully initialized. It's mainly used to access DOM elements and @ViewChild / @ViewChildren references, and to perform DOM-related operations.

Lifecycle hook

ngAfterViewChecked()

ngAfterViewChecked() runs after every change detection cycle for the component's view and child views. Used for tracking view updates or debugging — carefully, since it runs very often and can affect performance.

Lifecycle hook

ngOnDestroy()

ngOnDestroy() runs just before a component is destroyed. It's mainly used for cleanup — unsubscribing from Observables, removing event listeners, and clearing setInterval / setTimeout — to prevent memory leaks. See Avoiding memory leaks.

07

Change detection & Zone.js

Core concept

What is Change Detection in Angular?

Change detection is the process by which Angular checks component data and updates the DOM when something has changed. It keeps the UI in sync with the data — when data changes, Angular updates the view.

Strategies

Default vs. OnPush change detection

Angular provides two change detection strategies.

  • Default — checks the entire component tree on every change detection cycle, even if nothing changed. This can cause performance issues in large apps.
  • OnPush — tells Angular "don't check this component on every cycle — check it only when its inputs actually change, an event occurs, or an Observable emits." OnPush works on reference change, not value mutation.
Performance

Why is OnPush faster?

Because it reduces unnecessary checks by relying on immutable data and input reference changes, instead of checking the whole component tree every cycle.

Under the hood

What is zone.js?

Zone.js is a library that helps Angular automatically detect and update the view when something changes in the app — like when data is fetched, a timer finishes, or a user clicks a button.

  • Tracks asynchronous operations like HTTP calls and setTimeout.
  • Lets Angular know when these tasks are done, so it can run change detection and update the view.

Basically, zone.js saves developers from having to manually refresh the UI after an async action.

Service

What is NgZone?

NgZone is an Angular service, built on Zone.js, that tracks asynchronous operations — API calls, events, timers — and automatically triggers change detection to update the UI.

  • Something async happens (API call, click, timeout) → NgZone tells Angular "data changed, update the UI."
  • Also lets us run code outside Angular's zone, to improve performance.

Developers mostly use NgZone directly for performance optimization, working with third-party libraries, and avoiding unnecessary change detection.

Performance

runOutsideAngular()

runOutsideAngular() runs code outside Angular's zone, so it does not trigger change detection or UI updates. Good for performance-heavy tasks that don't need to update the view.

this.zone.runOutsideAngular(() => {
  // heavy work that shouldn't trigger change detection
});
Performance

ngZone.run()

ngZone.run() executes code back inside Angular's zone, so change detection runs and the UI updates. Use it when code is running outside Angular and you need to update the UI or navigate.

this.zone.run(() => {
  // update UI or navigate here
});
Modern Angular

What is Zone-less Angular?

Zone-less Angular means running Angular without Zone.js — without automatic change detection. Instead of Angular updating the UI automatically, you decide when to update it.

  • Used for better performance, and to avoid unnecessary change detection.
OnPush tools

detectChanges() vs. markForCheck()

Both are used with ChangeDetectionStrategy.OnPush, to update the UI manually.

detectChanges() immediately triggers change detection and updates the UI instantly. Use it when data comes from outside Angular — like setTimeout, a third-party library callback, or a manual DOM event — and you want an immediate UI update.

constructor(private cd: ChangeDetectorRef) {}

updateData() {
  this.name = 'Saurabh';
  this.cd.detectChanges();
}

markForCheck() does not update immediately — it only tells Angular "check this component in the next change detection cycle." Use it in OnPush components when data changes asynchronously, and you want Angular to recheck efficiently without forcing an immediate update.

constructor(private cd: ChangeDetectorRef) {}

updateData() {
  this.name = 'Saurabh';
  this.cd.markForCheck();
}
Part C · Templates, directives, pipes & forms
Templates, directives, pipes & forms

Everything that shapes what ends up in the DOM.

08

Templates, rendering & compilation

Component metadata

What is a Selector?

A selector is used to identify and load a component in HTML. It acts as a custom HTML tag through which Angular renders the component — the selector is the component's HTML tag name.

Component metadata

template vs. templateUrl

Both define a component's HTML.

template — the HTML is written directly inside the component.

@Component({
  selector: 'app-home',
  template: `<h1>Hello</h1>`
})

templateUrl — the HTML is written in a separate file.

@Component({
  selector: 'app-home',
  templateUrl: './home.component.html'
})

In real projects, templateUrl is more common, because it's easier to maintain.

Styling

What is View Encapsulation?

View Encapsulation controls how a component's CSS is applied — whether its styles stay inside it, or affect other components too. By default, Angular uses Emulated encapsulation, which keeps styles scoped to the component and prevents conflicts.

  • Emulated (default) — Angular adds unique attributes to scope styles; styles stay inside the component, preventing conflicts.
  • None — no encapsulation; styles become global, and can affect other components.
  • ShadowDom — uses the browser's Shadow DOM for full, strong style isolation.
@Component({
  selector: 'app-demo',
  templateUrl: './demo.html',
  styleUrls: ['./demo.css'],
  encapsulation: ViewEncapsulation.Emulated
})
Angular 17+

@defer in Angular

@defer is used to lazy-load parts of a template only when needed, instead of loading everything at once — reducing initial bundle size and improving performance.

  • on viewport — load when the block becomes visible.
  • on interaction — load on click / hover.
  • on timer — load after a set time.
  • on immediate — load right away, but defer execution.
@defer (on viewport) {
  <app-chart />
} @placeholder {
  <p>Loading...</p>
} @loading {
  <p>Please wait...</p>
} @error {
  <p>Error loading component</p>
}
Example: a homepage with a navbar, dashboard, charts, and a heavy analytics component. Without deferred loading, everything loads immediately and the page becomes slow. With it, the navbar and dashboard load first, and the heavy parts load later — on scroll, after a delay, or on a button click.
Advanced DI

What is forwardRef?

forwardRef tells Angular to resolve a dependency later, once the referenced class is fully available. It's mostly used when building custom form controls with ControlValueAccessor.

Imagine Angular reading your code top to bottom: it sees useExisting: MyInputComponent, but MyInputComponent isn't fully created yet. forwardRef(() => MyInputComponent) tells Angular: don't look for it now — look for it later, once everything is created.

Compilation

JIT vs. AOT compilation

Angular code (HTML + TypeScript) has to be converted into JavaScript the browser understands — this conversion is called compilation.

JIT (Just-In-Time) — code is compiled in the browser, at runtime. Angular uses JIT by default in development.

  • Good for: development, debugging, testing.
  • Downsides: slower app load, larger bundle size, less secure (the template is exposed).

AOT (Ahead-Of-Time) — code is compiled during the build, before the app runs. The browser gets ready-to-run JavaScript, with no compilation in the browser.

  • Good for: production builds, performance-sensitive and large apps.
  • Benefits: faster load, better performance, smaller bundle.

Angular needs AOT because it removes runtime compilation, improves performance, catches errors at build time, and makes the app more secure.

Compiler

What is Ivy?

Ivy is Angular's modern compiler and rendering engine — faster, smaller, and a better Angular engine. It converts Angular code into efficient JavaScript instructions to render the UI.

  • Makes Angular apps faster, and reduces bundle size.
  • Improves debugging and build performance, without changing how developers write Angular code.
  • Enabled by default since Angular 12.
Build optimization

What is Tree Shaking?

Tree shaking is a build optimization that automatically removes unused code from the final bundle — Angular only keeps the code you actually use.

  • Reduces bundle size, and improves Angular app performance.
  • Makes applications smaller and faster to load.
09

Pipes & directives

Template feature

What is a Pipe?

A Pipe transforms or formats data before showing it in the UI. Pipes are used only in templates, and they change how the data is displayed — not the original value.

  • Built-in examples: date, currency, uppercase.
Template feature

Pure Pipe vs. Impure Pipe

A pure pipe runs only when its input value changes, so it gives better performance — most built-in pipes (date, currency, uppercase) are pure by default.

An impure pipe runs on every change detection cycle, even if the input value didn't change. Custom pipes can be marked impure, and should be used carefully since they can affect performance.

RxJS

Why use pipe() before subscribe()?

pipe() lets us apply RxJS operators — like map, filter, debounceTime, or distinctUntilChanged — to process the observable's data before it reaches subscribe().

  • Filter data, delay data, transform data, avoid duplicate API calls, and handle errors — all before subscribe().
Template feature

What are Directives in Angular?

Directives change the behavior or appearance of DOM elements. Angular provides built-in directives like ngIf, ngFor, and ngClass.

  • Component directive — every Angular component is a directive (@Component()).
  • Structural directive — changes the DOM structure, like *ngIf and *ngFor.
  • Attribute directive — changes an element's style or behavior, like ngClass and ngStyle.
Directive

Structural Directives

Structural directives change the structure of the DOM — adding or removing elements — like *ngIf and *ngFor.

Directive

Attribute Directives

Attribute directives change the appearance or behavior of an existing element, without adding or removing it — like ngClass, ngStyle, and ngModel.

Example

Custom Directive example

A custom directive can add its own behavior to an element — for example, a highlight directive that turns the background yellow when the mouse hovers over it.

import { Directive, ElementRef, HostListener } from '@angular/core';

@Directive({
  selector: '[appHighlight]'
})
export class HighlightDirective {
  constructor(private el: ElementRef) {}

  @HostListener('mouseenter')
  onMouseEnter() {
    this.el.nativeElement.style.backgroundColor = 'yellow';
  }

  @HostListener('mouseleave')
  onMouseLeave() {
    this.el.nativeElement.style.backgroundColor = '';
  }
}
<p appHighlight>Move mouse here</p>
10

Forms

Forms

Reactive Forms vs. Template-driven Forms

Reactive forms build form inputs using TypeScript code — FormGroup, FormControl, and Validators — giving better control and validation for complex, dynamic forms.

Template-driven forms write the logic in HTML — Angular creates the form model, and validation lives in the template. Suitable for simple forms.

Reactive Forms

FormGroup

FormGroup groups multiple form controls together, and represents the form as one object. Use it for a fixed set of fields — like a login or registration form.

Reactive Forms

FormArray

FormArray manages a dynamic list of controls as an array — for fields that repeat, like multiple skills, multiple addresses, or dynamic table rows.

Reactive Forms

Dynamic Forms

Dynamic forms are generated at runtime from a configuration object, JSON, or API data, instead of hardcoding fields in the HTML. FormControls are created dynamically, and the template loops over them to render the inputs.

Use case: in enterprise apps like an ERP, this is more scalable when different forms have different fields, or the structure can change without touching the UI code.
11

Forms deep dive

Reactive Forms

Custom Validators

A custom validator is a function that checks a form control's value and returns an error object if it's invalid, or null if it's valid. Use one when the built-in validators — required, minLength, pattern — aren't enough.

export function noSpecialChars(control: AbstractControl): ValidationErrors | null {
  const valid = /^[a-zA-Z0-9]*$/.test(control.value);
  return valid ? null : { specialChars: true };
}
name = new FormControl('', [Validators.required, noSpecialChars]);
Reactive Forms

Async Validators

An async validator runs an asynchronous check — like calling an API to see if a username is already taken — and returns an Observable or Promise of the validation result, instead of a plain object.

export function uniqueUsernameValidator(userService: UserService): AsyncValidatorFn {
  return (control: AbstractControl) =>
    userService.checkUsername(control.value).pipe(
      map(isTaken => (isTaken ? { usernameTaken: true } : null))
    );
}
Common use: checking whether an email or username already exists, before letting the user submit.
Reactive Forms

Cross-field validation

Cross-field validation compares two or more fields together — like confirming that password and confirmPassword match. It's added at the FormGroup level, not on a single control.

export function passwordsMatch(group: AbstractControl): ValidationErrors | null {
  const pass = group.get('password')?.value;
  const confirm = group.get('confirmPassword')?.value;
  return pass === confirm ? null : { passwordsMismatch: true };
}
this.form = new FormGroup({
  password: new FormControl(''),
  confirmPassword: new FormControl('')
}, { validators: passwordsMatch });
Reactive Forms

FormBuilder

FormBuilder is a helper service that cuts down the boilerplate of creating FormGroups, FormControls, and FormArrays by hand.

constructor(private fb: FormBuilder) {}

form = this.fb.group({
  name: ['', Validators.required],
  email: ['', [Validators.required, Validators.email]]
});
Custom form controls

ControlValueAccessor

ControlValueAccessor is the interface Angular uses to let a custom component work with [(ngModel)] or formControlName, just like a native input. Implementing it lets a component — like a star rating or a custom date picker — plug directly into Reactive or Template-driven forms.

  • writeValue() — Angular calls this to set the value from outside.
  • registerOnChange() / registerOnTouched() — register the callbacks Angular uses to know when the value or touched state changes.
  • setDisabledState() — called when the form disables or enables the control.

It's commonly paired with forwardRef(), since Angular needs to reference the component's own class before it's fully defined.

Part D · Routing & dependency injection
Routing & dependency injection

Navigating between views, and wiring services together.

12

Routing & navigation

Navigation

What is Angular Routing?

Angular routing allows navigation between different components in a single page application. Each route maps a URL path to a specific component.

  • Supports lazy loading, guards, and parameters.
  • Navigation can be done using routerLink, or the Router service.
Performance

Lazy Loading, Eager Loading & Preloading

Lazy loading means loading a feature module only when the user navigates to that route, instead of loading the entire application at once. It reduces the initial bundle size, and improves the first load time.

Eager loading loads everything at startup. It's simpler, but leads to a slower initial load — fine for small apps.

Preloading strategies load lazy modules in the background, after the app has already started, so there's no delay the next time the user navigates there.

Navigation

Route Guards in Angular

Route guards are functions that control whether navigation to — or away from — a route is allowed.

  • CanActivate — allows or blocks navigation to a route.
  • CanActivateChild — protects child routes.
  • CanMatch — decides whether a route should match, before Angular loads it.
  • CanDeactivate — asks for confirmation before leaving a form or page.
  • Resolve — pre-fetches data before the route loads.
Guard

AuthGuard

AuthGuard is a route guard that checks whether a user is allowed to access a route. It protects private pages, prevents unauthorized access, and improves security.

Guard

What is a Resolver in Angular?

A resolver is a route guard that preloads the data a route needs, before navigating to it. Angular waits for the data first, then loads the component — instead of showing an empty screen or a loading state.

Step 1: create the resolver

import { ResolveFn } from '@angular/router';
import { inject } from '@angular/core';
import { EmployeeService } from './employee.service';

export const employeeResolver: ResolveFn<any> = (route) => {
  const service = inject(EmployeeService);
  return service.getEmployee(route.paramMap.get('id')!);
};

Step 2: add it to the route

{
  path: 'employee/:id',
  component: EmployeeComponent,
  resolve: {
    employee: employeeResolver
  }
}
Guard

What is CanMatch?

CanMatch decides whether a route should even match, before Angular loads it. It's commonly used with lazy-loaded routes for role-based access, and is the recommended replacement for the older CanLoad.

Example: in an ERP application, only Admin users should access the /admin route. CanMatch checks the user's role first — if they're an Admin, Angular loads the route; otherwise it redirects to another page, like /login.
// AdminGuard.ts
import { CanMatchFn, Router } from '@angular/router';
import { inject } from '@angular/core';
import { AuthService } from './auth.service';

export const adminGuard: CanMatchFn = () => {
  const authService = inject(AuthService);
  const router = inject(Router);

  if (authService.isAdmin()) {
    return true;
  }
  return router.createUrlTree(['/login']);
};
// route.ts
{
  path: 'admin',
  loadChildren: () =>
    import('./admin/admin.routes').then(m => m.routes),
  canMatch: [adminGuard]
}
Guard

CanDeactivate

CanDeactivate controls whether a user can leave the current page — commonly used to warn about unsaved changes.

Example: in an ERP application, a user edits a form and tries to navigate away without saving. CanDeactivate shows a confirmation dialog — "Are you sure you want to leave?" If the user confirms, Angular navigates away; otherwise, it keeps them on the current page.
Guard

CanActivateChild

CanActivateChild protects every child route of a parent route at once, instead of adding CanActivate to each child route individually.

Example: in an ERP application, all pages under /admin — /admin/users, /admin/reports, /admin/settings — can be protected with a single CanActivateChild, so only authorized users can access any of them.
// admin-route.ts
{
  path: 'admin',
  canActivateChild: [adminGuard],
  children: [
    { path: 'dashboard', component: DashboardComponent },
    { path: 'users', component: UsersComponent },
    { path: 'reports', component: ReportsComponent },
    { path: 'settings', component: SettingsComponent }
  ]
}
Navigation

Query Parameters

Query parameters are values passed in the URL, commonly used for pagination, filters, search, sorting, or tab state — e.g. /users?page=1&role=admin.

Setting query params

this.router.navigate(
  ['/users'],
  {
    queryParams: {
      page: 1,
      role: 'admin'
    }
  }
);

Getting query params

this.route.queryParams.subscribe(params => {
  console.log(params['page']);
  console.log(params['role']);
});
Security

Role-based access in Angular

Role-based access is usually implemented with route guards and an authentication service. After login, the user's role is stored — in a token, local storage, or app state — and a guard checks that role before allowing access to a route.

Flow: login → get the user's role from the API/token → store the role → the guard checks the role → allow or block the route.

Routing

Wildcard Route (**)

A wildcard route (path: '**') catches any URL that doesn't match a defined route — typically used to show a 404 / Page Not Found component, or to redirect somewhere.

{ path: '**', component: PageNotFoundComponent }

It must be placed last in the route configuration, since Angular checks routes top to bottom — if ** came first, it would match every URL, and the routes after it would never be reached.

Routing

Default Route

A default route defines which page opens when the user visits the app at an empty path (''). pathMatch: 'full' makes sure the redirect only happens when the URL is completely empty.

const routes: Routes = [
  { path: '', redirectTo: 'dashboard', pathMatch: 'full' },
  { path: 'dashboard', component: DashboardComponent },
  { path: '**', component: PageNotFoundComponent }
];
Routing

Storing data in routes

Angular routes have a data property, where we can store static data — like a page title, a role, or breadcrumbs.

const routes: Routes = [
  {
    path: 'dashboard',
    component: DashboardComponent,
    data: {
      title: 'Dashboard',
      role: 'Admin'
    }
  }
];
// Access it in the component
this.route.snapshot.data['title'];
13

Service & Dependency Injection

Design pattern

What is Dependency Injection?

Dependency Injection (DI) is a design pattern used in Angular to provide services to components. Instead of creating objects manually, Angular automatically creates and injects the required service.

  • The component does not create the service itself — Angular creates it and injects it.
  • Helps code reuse, and makes applications easier to test and maintain.
Real-world example: Service = the pizza (the business logic). Dependency Injection = Swiggy or Zomato — the system that delivers the pizza to you. You don't cook the pizza yourself, so you don't create services yourself — Angular delivers it for you.
Decorator

@Injectable()

@Injectable() is a decorator that marks a class as available for Angular's Dependency Injection system. It lets Angular create the service, inject its own dependencies — such as HttpClient or Router — and manage its lifecycle. 

@Injectable({
  providedIn: 'root'
})
export class UserService {}

With providedIn: 'root', Angular registers the service in the root injector, creates one shared (singleton) instance for the whole application, and makes it tree-shakable — if the service is never used, Angular removes it from the production bundle.

Edge case

Can a service exist without @Injectable()?

Yes — but only if it has no constructor dependencies, and you don't expect Angular to inject anything into it.

Building block

What is a Service in Angular?

A Service in Angular is a reusable TypeScript class that contains business logic, data handling, API calls, or other shared functionality. It is injected into components using Dependency Injection.

  • Helps avoid duplicate code.
  • Keeps components focused on the UI.
  • Improves maintainability and reusability.
DI configuration

What are Providers in Angular?

A provider tells Angular how to create and supply a service. When a component needs a service, the provider creates the instance and injects it into the component.

  • Can be configured at the root, module, or component level.
  • Support different strategies: useClass, useValue, and useFactory.
One shared instance

What is a Singleton Service in Angular?

A singleton service means only one instance of the service is created and shared across the entire application — one instance used everywhere in the app.

This is usually achieved with providedIn: 'root', which registers the service in the root injector so it can be shared across all components.

Comparison

providedIn vs. providers

Both providedIn and providers are used for Dependency Injection in Angular. The main difference is where the service is registered, and how many instances get created.

providedIn

  • Added inside the service itself, using @Injectable().
  • providedIn: 'root' creates one shared instance for the whole application (singleton).
  • Supports tree-shaking — if the service is never used, Angular removes it from the final bundle.
@Injectable({
  providedIn: 'root'
})
export class UserService {}

providers

  • Added in a component, directive, route, or app configuration.
  • Angular creates a new instance for that scope.
  • Different components can have different instances of the same service.
  • Not tree-shakable like providedIn.
@Component({
  selector: 'app-home',
  providers: [UserService]
})
export class HomeComponent {}
Advanced DI

What is the Multi Provider Token?

Multi Provider Token means many services can be added under one token. Angular stores them in an array, instead of replacing the previous provider.

  • multi: true tells Angular to keep multiple providers together, instead of replacing the old one.
providers: [
  {
    provide: HTTP_INTERCEPTORS,
    useClass: AuthInterceptor,
    multi: true
  },
]
14

Advanced DI & DOM APIs

Dependency Injection

@Host(), @Self(), @SkipSelf(), @Optional()

These decorators fine-tune where Angular looks for a dependency in the injector tree.

  • @Self() — only look in this component's own injector, not any parent.
  • @SkipSelf() — skip this component's own injector, and look starting from the parent.
  • @Host() — stop searching at the host component, without going further up to the app root.
  • @Optional() — don't throw an error if the dependency isn't found; inject null instead.
constructor(@Optional() @Self() private logger: LoggerService) {}
Directives

@HostBinding()

@HostBinding() binds a property of the host element — a class, style, or attribute — to a property on the directive or component class.

@HostBinding('class.active') isActive = false;
@HostBinding('style.color') textColor = 'blue';
Directives

@HostListener()

@HostListener() listens for an event on the host element — like click, mouseenter, or a window event — and runs a method when it fires. See the custom directive example.

@HostListener('window:resize', ['$event'])
onResize(event: Event) {
  console.log('window resized');
}
DOM access

Renderer2 vs. ElementRef

ElementRef gives direct access to the native DOM element. Renderer2 is Angular's preferred way to change the DOM instead, because it works safely across platforms — browser, server-side rendering, web workers — and avoids touching the DOM directly.

constructor(private el: ElementRef, private renderer: Renderer2) {}

ngOnInit() {
  this.renderer.setStyle(this.el.nativeElement, 'color', 'red');
}

Direct DOM manipulation through el.nativeElement.style... works, but isn't safe outside the browser — e.g. under SSR — so Renderer2 is the recommended approach.

Directives

exportAs

exportAs gives a directive a name that can be referenced with a template reference variable, so a template can access the directive's public properties and methods directly.

@Directive({
  selector: '[appTooltip]',
  exportAs: 'appTooltip'
})
export class TooltipDirective {
  show() { /* ... */ }
}
<button appTooltip #t="appTooltip" (click)="t.show()">Hover me</button>
Part E · HTTP, performance, security & testing
HTTP, performance, security & testing

Talking to a server, and keeping the app fast, safe, and tested.

15

HTTP, interceptors & errors

HTTP

What is HttpClient?

HttpClient is an Angular service used to communicate with a backend server. It sends HTTP requests — GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, PATCH — and returns the response as an RxJS Observable.

HTTP

How to call an API in Angular

APIs are usually called inside a service, using HttpClient.

  • Provide HttpClient (or import HttpClientModule).
  • Inject HttpClient into the service's constructor.
  • Use methods like get(), post(), put(), or delete() — each returns an Observable.
  • The component subscribes (or uses the async pipe) to get the data.
HTTP

What is an Interceptor?

An interceptor is a service that sits in the middle of every HTTP request and response, and can modify them globally — without changing every individual API call.

  • Adding a JWT token or headers automatically, instead of adding it to every request by hand.
  • Global error handling — e.g. redirecting to login on a 401, in one place.
  • Logging API calls.
  • Caching a response, to avoid calling the same API repeatedly (e.g. shared master data).
  • Showing or hiding a loading spinner.
Error handling

How to handle API errors in Angular

API errors are usually handled with the RxJS catchError operator inside a service, and with HTTP interceptors for global cases like authentication errors or server issues. In the component, we display a user-friendly message based on the error.

Error handling

Handling 404 / 500 error pages

For a 404 (page not found), Angular's wildcard route (**) redirects to a Not Found page. For a 500 (server error) — an API failing, or the server crashing — an HTTP interceptor can catch it and navigate to a custom Server Error page.

16

Performance & memory

Cleanup

How to avoid memory leaks in Angular

A memory leak happens when Angular removes a component from the screen, but subscriptions, timers, or listeners keep running in memory — RxJS subscriptions, setInterval/setTimeout, DOM event listeners, WebSocket connections. Over time, this increases memory use, can trigger duplicate API calls, and slows the app down.

1. Unsubscribe in ngOnDestroy

subscription!: Subscription;

ngOnInit() {
  this.subscription = this.userService.getUsers().subscribe();
}

ngOnDestroy() {
  this.subscription.unsubscribe();
}

2. takeUntil (a common enterprise pattern)

private destroy$ = new Subject<void>();

ngOnInit() {
  this.userService.getUsers()
    .pipe(takeUntil(this.destroy$))
    .subscribe();
}

ngOnDestroy() {
  this.destroy$.next();
  this.destroy$.complete();
}

3. takeUntilDestroyed() (Angular 16+, recommended) — Angular automatically handles cleanup when the component is destroyed.

import { takeUntilDestroyed } from '@angular/core/rxjs-interop';
import { DestroyRef, inject } from '@angular/core';

destroyRef = inject(DestroyRef);

ngOnInit() {
  this.userService.getUsers()
    .pipe(takeUntilDestroyed(this.destroyRef))
    .subscribe();
}

4. Async pipe (best when possible) — subscribes and unsubscribes automatically, no manual cleanup needed.

<div>{{ users$ | async | json }}</div>

5. Angular Signals — don't require manual subscriptions; Angular manages the lifecycle automatically.

users = toSignal(this.userService.getUsers());

In real projects, the takeUntil pattern is common wherever a component has multiple RxJS subscriptions — like API calls and form valueChanges — alongside the async pipe in templates, since Angular unsubscribes automatically there.

Diagnostics

How to check Angular project performance

Performance is usually checked with browser developer tools, Angular DevTools, Lighthouse, and bundle analyzers — monitoring change detection cycles, bundle size, API calls, and rendering performance.

Angular DevTools (Chrome extension) shows component rendering time, change detection cycles, the component hierarchy, and performance bottlenecks — if one component re-renders 100 times unnecessarily, it shows up here.

Chrome DevTools Performance tab: press F12 → Performance → Record → use the app → stop recording. Check JavaScript execution time, rendering time, repaints, long tasks, and memory usage.

Network tab: F12 → Network. Check API response times, duplicate API calls, large payloads, and failed requests.

Lighthouse audit: F12 → Lighthouse → Analyze page load. Measures performance score, accessibility, best practices, and SEO.

Bundle size: build for production, then analyze the output.

ng build --configuration production
npm install -g source-map-explorer
source-map-explorer dist/*.js

This shows which libraries take up more space, large bundles, and unused code.

Optimization

How to optimize Angular performance

  • Use OnPush change detection — checks only when input changes.
  • Use trackBy in *ngFor — prevents re-rendering the full list.
  • Use lazy loading — load modules or routes only when the user visits that page.
  • Use the async pipe — Angular handles subscribe/unsubscribe automatically.
  • Use pure pipes.
  • Lazy-load images (loading="lazy").
  • Cache API responses to avoid unnecessary calls (see shareReplay).
  • Split code into smaller, reusable pieces.
  • Unsubscribe Observables with takeUntil or takeUntilDestroyed, and clear intervals.
*ngFor

trackBy in *ngFor

trackBy is a function used with *ngFor to improve performance. It tells Angular how to uniquely identify each item in a list, so when the list changes, Angular updates only the changed items instead of re-rendering the whole list.

17

Security, environments & tooling

Security

XSS protection & DomSanitizer

Angular automatically sanitizes values bound into the DOM — HTML, styles, URLs, resource URLs — to protect against cross-site scripting (XSS) attacks. It strips out anything it considers unsafe before rendering.

When we genuinely trust a value — like HTML from our own CMS — we can bypass sanitization for that one value using DomSanitizer.

constructor(private sanitizer: DomSanitizer) {}

trustedHtml = this.sanitizer.bypassSecurityTrustHtml(rawHtmlFromCms);

Use bypassSecurityTrustHtml / Url / Style / Script sparingly, and only for content you actually trust — it turns off Angular's protection for that value.

Configuration

environment.ts files

Angular projects keep separate environment files — like environment.ts for development and environment.prod.ts for production — holding values that change between environments, such as an API base URL or feature flags.

// environment.ts
export const environment = {
  production: false,
  apiUrl: 'http://localhost:3000/api'
};

angular.json's file replacements swap in the right file automatically for each build configuration, so the rest of the code just imports environment without caring which one it got.

Tooling

Common Angular CLI commands

  • ng generate component name — scaffolds a new component (also ng g c).
  • ng serve — runs the app locally with live reload.
  • ng build --configuration production — builds an optimized production bundle.
  • ng test — runs unit tests.
  • ng add <package> — installs and configures a library, e.g. Angular Material.
  • ng update — upgrades Angular and its dependencies.
18

Testing in Angular

Unit testing

What is TestBed?

TestBed is Angular's testing utility that creates a mini Angular testing module — it lets us configure providers, imports, and declarations, then create components and inject services the same way Angular does at runtime.

TestBed.configureTestingModule({
  declarations: [UserCardComponent],
  providers: [UserService]
});
Unit testing

Testing a Component

A component test typically creates the component with TestBed, gets a ComponentFixture, and checks both the component instance and its rendered template.

let fixture: ComponentFixture<UserCardComponent>;
let component: UserCardComponent;

beforeEach(() => {
  fixture = TestBed.createComponent(UserCardComponent);
  component = fixture.componentInstance;
  fixture.detectChanges();
});

it('should display the user name', () => {
  component.user = { name: 'Saurabh' };
  fixture.detectChanges();
  const el: HTMLElement = fixture.nativeElement;
  expect(el.textContent).toContain('Saurabh');
});
Unit testing

Testing a Service

Services are usually simpler to test — inject the service from TestBed, and call its methods directly, without needing a component or the DOM.

let service: CartService;

beforeEach(() => {
  TestBed.configureTestingModule({ providers: [CartService] });
  service = TestBed.inject(CartService);
});

it('should add an item', () => {
  service.addItem({ id: 1, name: 'Book' });
  expect(service.items.length).toBe(1);
});
Unit testing

HttpTestingController

HttpTestingController lets us test code that calls HttpClient, without hitting a real API. We trigger the call, then assert on and respond to the fake HTTP request ourselves.

TestBed.configureTestingModule({
  imports: [HttpClientTestingModule],
  providers: [UserService]
});
const httpMock = TestBed.inject(HttpTestingController);
const service = TestBed.inject(UserService);

it('should fetch users', () => {
  service.getUsers().subscribe(users => {
    expect(users.length).toBe(2);
  });

  const req = httpMock.expectOne('/api/users');
  req.flush([{ id: 1 }, { id: 2 }]);
});
Unit testing

Mocking dependencies with spies

A spy replaces a real dependency's method with a fake one we control — so a component test doesn't need a real service, a real API call, or a real database.

const userServiceSpy = jasmine.createSpyObj('UserService', ['getUsers']);
userServiceSpy.getUsers.and.returnValue(of([{ id: 1, name: 'Ram' }]));

TestBed.configureTestingModule({
  providers: [{ provide: UserService, useValue: userServiceSpy }]
});

This keeps component tests fast and focused — they test the component's own logic, not the service's.

19

SSR & advanced DI

Rendering

SSR (Server-Side Rendering) in Angular

Normally, Angular renders the page in the browser. With SSR, Angular renders the page on the server first, and sends the ready HTML to the browser — so the user sees the page faster, and it's more SEO-friendly.

  • Without SSR: open the site → the browser loads Angular → builds the page → shows the page.
  • With SSR: open the site → the server sends ready HTML → the user sees the page immediately → Angular then takes over in the browser (hydration).

SSR in Angular is implemented using Angular Universal, which generates the HTML on the server before sending it to the browser.

Rendering

Hydration

After SSR sends a ready-made HTML page, hydration is what makes that page "alive" — adding the JavaScript that makes buttons, forms, and events actually work.

Without hydration, you can see the page, but can't interact with it — buttons don't work, forms can't submit. With hydration, everything works as normal.

Advanced DI

What is an InjectionToken?

InjectionToken is a unique key used to inject values that aren't classes — strings, objects, or configuration data — through Angular's Dependency Injection.

InjectionToken is used to create a unique DI token for values that don't have a class, such as strings, objects, or configuration. We can provide the value once and inject it wherever required.

Example: instead of hardcoding an API URL in every service, we create an API_URL InjectionToken, provide the URL once, and inject it wherever it's needed — making the app easy to reconfigure for different environments, like development, testing, and production.

Step 1: create a token

// api.token.ts
import { InjectionToken } from '@angular/core';

export const API_URL = new InjectionToken<string>('API_URL');

Step 2: provide the value

// app.config.ts
import { ApplicationConfig } from '@angular/core';
import { API_URL } from './api.token';

export const appConfig: ApplicationConfig = {
  providers: [
    {
      provide: API_URL,
      useValue: 'https://api.company.com'
    }
  ]
};

Step 3: use it inside a service

// user.service.ts
import { Injectable, inject } from '@angular/core';
import { API_URL } from './api.token';

@Injectable({
  providedIn: 'root'
})
export class UserService {
  private apiUrl = inject(API_URL);

  getUsers() {
    console.log(this.apiUrl);
  }
}
Part F · RxJS & reactive programming
RxJS & reactive programming

Handling data that arrives over time.

20

Foundations

Library

What is RxJS?

RxJS is a library used in Angular to handle asynchronous data. It is a reactive programming library based on Observables, and it helps us handle, transform, and control data that comes in over time.

  • Used for API calls, user input, events, and real-time data.
  • Everything else in this handbook is either a type of Observable, or an operator that works on one.
Foundation

Observable

Observable is a feature from RxJS, used to handle asynchronous data streams.

  • Used for API calls, user events, and real-time data.
  • Can return multiple values over time — unlike a Promise, which resolves once.
  • Can be cancelled mid-stream by calling unsubscribe().
const numbers$ = new Observable<number>(observer => {
  observer.next(1);
  observer.next(2);
  observer.complete();
});
Foundation

Observer

Observer = listener / receiver of data. It is a function or object that receives data from an Observable and reacts to it — the one that subscribes and handles the data, errors, and completion.

  • next — handles each emitted value.
  • error — handles a failure.
  • complete — runs once the stream finishes.
observable.subscribe({
  next: (value) => console.log(value),
  error: (err) => console.log(err),
  complete: () => console.log('Done')
});

Everything inside that subscribe() call is the Observer.

Design decision

Why does Angular use Observables instead of Promises?

Angular uses Observables because they support multiple values, cancellation, lazy execution, and powerful operators. These are important for handling HTTP calls, events, and real-time data.

  • Promises handle one-time async operations, and can't be cancelled.
  • Observables handle continuous, cancellable data streams — that's why they suit Angular better.
Practice

Handling asynchronous operations in Angular

In Angular, asynchronous operations are mainly handled using RxJS Observables together with HttpClient. We subscribe to Observables, or use the async pipe so subscribe and unsubscribe happen on their own.

  • For simple cases, we can also use Promises with async/await.
  • Observables are preferred because they support cancellation and multiple values.
21

Subjects

A Subject is a special Observable that can both send data and receive data. The four types below all differ in how much old data they remember for a subscriber who joins late.

Subject

Subject

Subject in RxJS is a special Observable that lets us both send data and receive data. It can have multiple subscribers, and it only sends values to the ones that are subscribed right now.

  • Does not store old values — only current subscribers get data.
  • Used for event communication, like a button click, a refresh, or a modal opening or closing.
private refresh$ = new Subject<void>();
refreshTriggered$ = this.refresh$.asObservable();

triggerRefresh() {
  this.refresh$.next();
}
Subject with memory

BehaviorSubject

BehaviorSubject stores the latest value and sends it immediately to new subscribers. It requires an initial value, which is what makes it ideal for state management — it always holds and emits the latest value.

  • Requires a starting value when it's created.
  • Used for state management: logged-in user, theme, or cart item count.
private userSubject = new BehaviorSubject<User | null>(null);
user$ = this.userSubject.asObservable();

setUser(user: User) {
  this.userSubject.next(user);
}
Subject with a buffer

ReplaySubject

ReplaySubject stores multiple previous values and sends them to new subscribers, not just the single latest one.

  • Stores multiple past values, not just one.
  • New subscribers receive the previous values too.
  • Useful for things like chat history or logs.
private log$ = new ReplaySubject<string>(5); // keeps the last 5 entries

addLog(entry: string) {
  this.log$.next(entry);
}
// a component that subscribes later still receives the last 5 lines
Final-value only

AsyncSubject

AsyncSubject emits only the last value, and only when the Observable completes. It stores the latest value and sends it to subscribers only after complete() is called.

  • Ignores every value in between — only the final one matters.
  • Good for scenarios where we only need the final result after a process finishes.
upload$ = new AsyncSubject<string>();

startUpload() {
  this.upload$.next('Uploading...');
  this.upload$.next('Almost Done...');
  this.upload$.next('Upload Completed');
  this.upload$.complete();
}
// subscribers only ever see: 'Upload Completed'
Example: suppose a file upload gives progress — 10%, 30%, 60%, 100% — but the UI only needs the final result. AsyncSubject is useful for exactly that.
22

Transformation operators

These four operators all switch to a new Observable when a new value comes in. They differ in what happens if a new value arrives before the old one has finished.

Transformation

switchMap()

switchMap() is an RxJS operator used to switch to a new Observable and cancel the previous one. It cancels the previous Observable and subscribes to the latest one.

  • Useful when multiple API calls are triggered continuously, like in a search box, where we only care about the latest result.
  • Helps avoid unnecessary calls and race conditions.
this.searchControl.valueChanges.pipe(
  switchMap(term => this.api.search(term))
).subscribe(results => this.results = results);
// a new keystroke cancels the in-flight request for the previous one
Use case: a search box, where multiple calls fire in quick succession but only the latest result matters.
Transformation

mergeMap()

mergeMap() is an RxJS operator used to handle multiple Observables in parallel. It subscribes to all inner Observables and runs them at the same time, without cancelling any of them.

  • Runs multiple API calls at the same time and processes every result.
uploadClicks$.pipe(
  mergeMap(file => this.api.upload(file))
).subscribe(res => console.log('uploaded', res));
// every file uploads in parallel — nothing waits on anything else
Use case: uploading several files together.
Transformation

concatMap()

concatMap() is an RxJS operator used to run Observables one by one, in sequence. It waits for each one to complete before starting the next.

  • Keeps everything in order — nothing overlaps.
this.saveForm().pipe(
  concatMap(() => this.api.getUpdatedList())
).subscribe(list => this.list = list);
// getUpdatedList only fires once the save has fully completed
Use case: save form data, then — only after it succeeds — fetch the refreshed list.
Transformation

exhaustMap()

exhaustMap() is an RxJS operator used to ignore new requests while one is already running.

  • Prevents duplicate API calls.
  • Avoids multiple clicks or double form submissions.
submitClicks$.pipe(
  exhaustMap(() => this.api.submitForm())
).subscribe();
// clicks while a submit is in flight are dropped, not queued
Use case: a user clicks Submit five times — only the first request is processed, the rest are ignored.
In short: switchMap cancels previous calls, mergeMap handles parallel requests, concatMap ensures execution in order, exhaustMap ignores new requests until the previous one completes, and takeUntil is used for automatic cleanup and to avoid memory leaks.
23

Filtering & cleanup

Timing

debounceTime()

debounceTime() waits for a pause before emitting the latest value. It is used to avoid unnecessary API calls and reduce how often frequent events fire.

  • Commonly used in search inputs to avoid multiple API calls and improve performance.
  • Often paired with distinctUntilChanged and switchMap in a search box.
this.searchControl.valueChanges.pipe(
  debounceTime(300),
  distinctUntilChanged(),
  switchMap(term => this.searchService.search(term))
).subscribe(results => this.results = results);
Example: user types quickly: a → ab → abc. Without debounce, that's 3 API calls. With debounce, only 1 API call fires — after the pause.
Timing

throttleTime()

throttleTime() is an RxJS operator that emits the first value immediately, and then ignores the next values for a specific time.

  • Use it to limit execution rate and control frequent events.
fromEvent(window, 'scroll').pipe(
  throttleTime(200)
).subscribe(() => this.onScroll());
Use case: scroll or button click. User scrolls continuously — with throttle, we only get a limited number of events instead of every single one.
Filter

distinctUntilChanged()

distinctUntilChanged() emits a value only when it is different from the previous emitted value. In simple words — if the value is the same as the previous one, it is ignored.

  • Avoids duplicate API calls.
  • Improves performance and reduces unnecessary processing.
Example: user types in search: Angular, Angular, Angular, React. Without distinctUntilChanged(), the API is called 4 times. With it, the API is called only 2 times — for Angular and React — because the repeated "Angular" is the same as the value before it.
Cleanup — classic

takeUntil()

takeUntil is used to automatically unsubscribe from an Observable when another Observable emits a value. It is commonly used in Angular to clean up subscriptions in ngOnDestroy and prevent memory leaks. This is the old way — you create your own Subject and complete it manually.

import { Subject, takeUntil } from 'rxjs';

export class UserComponent implements OnDestroy {
  private destroy$ = new Subject<void>();

  ngOnInit() {
    this.userService.getUsers()
      .pipe(takeUntil(this.destroy$))
      .subscribe();
  }

  ngOnDestroy() {
    this.destroy$.next();
    this.destroy$.complete();
  }
}

Problem with this approach: extra code — we need a Subject, we need ngOnDestroy, and it's easy to forget the cleanup.

Cleanup — modern

takeUntilDestroyed()

takeUntilDestroyed was introduced in Angular 16. It automatically unsubscribes when the component is destroyed, and needs less code than the old way.

import { takeUntilDestroyed } from '@angular/core/rxjs-interop';

export class UserComponent {
  private destroyRef = inject(DestroyRef);

  ngOnInit() {
    this.userService.getUsers()
      .pipe(takeUntilDestroyed(this.destroyRef))
      .subscribe();
  }
}

Benefits: no Subject, no ngOnDestroy, less code, automatic cleanup — this is the recommended approach in Angular 16+.

24

Combination operators

Combination

combineLatest()

combineLatest() combines multiple Observables and emits the latest values whenever any one of them changes.

  • Used when the UI depends on multiple inputs and needs the latest values from all streams.
combineLatest([searchText$, filter$]).subscribe(([text, filter]) => {
  this.results = applyFilter(text, filter);
});
Use case: search text plus a filter dropdown. When the user changes the search text or the filter, the UI updates using the latest values from both.
Combination

forkJoin()

forkJoin() is used to run multiple Observables in parallel and get the results only when all of them complete. It works like Promise.all().

  • Use it when multiple API calls are needed and we need all results together.
  • Returns the combined result as one object, once every source has finished.
ngOnInit() {
  forkJoin({
    users: this.apiService.getUsers(),
    products: this.apiService.getProducts(),
    orders: this.apiService.getOrders()
  }).subscribe({
    next: (res) => {
      console.log(res.users);
      console.log(res.products);
      console.log(res.orders);
    },
    error: (err) => console.log(err)
  });
}
Use case: handling multiple API calls on page load. forkJoin is the most common choice here — it runs the calls in parallel and returns all responses together, once they've all finished.
Combination

zip()

zip() combines multiple Observables by pairing values that are at the same index. It waits for each Observable to emit one value, then emits them together as a pair.

zip(questions$, answers$).subscribe(([question, answer]) => {
  console.log(question, '->', answer);
});
Use case: a quiz app with questions and answers, paired up one to one.
Combination

withLatestFrom()

withLatestFrom is used when we want to get the latest value from another Observable, whenever the main Observable emits. The second stream doesn't trigger anything on its own — it just gives its latest value along for the ride.

loginClicked$.pipe(
  withLatestFrom(username$)
).subscribe(([_, username]) => {
  console.log('Login with', username);
});
Example: a Login button and a username field. Username typed: Ram, Saurabh, John. When the Login button is clicked, we want the latest username. Login button = main Observable, username = latest-value Observable. withLatestFrom says: when the login button is clicked, give me the latest username. Output: "Login with John".
Combination

race()

race is used to take the value from whichever Observable emits first. The fastest Observable wins.

import { race, timer } from 'rxjs';
import { mapTo } from 'rxjs/operators';

const obs1 = timer(2000).pipe(mapTo('API 1'));
const obs2 = timer(1000).pipe(mapTo('API 2'));

race(obs1, obs2).subscribe(data => console.log(data));
// logs 'API 2' — it resolved first
25

Utility & error handling

Utility

tap()

tap is used for side effects. It does not change the data — it just lets you look at it or react to it along the way.

import { of } from 'rxjs';
import { tap } from 'rxjs/operators';

of(10, 20).pipe(
  tap(value => console.log('Before:', value))
).subscribe(data => {
  console.log('Final:', data);
});

Mostly used for: console logging, debugging, and checking the API response. Also used to show a loader (often paired with finalize to hide it again).

Error handling

catchError()

catchError() is an RxJS operator used to catch and handle errors in an Observable. It lets us show an error message, log the error, or return fallback data, so the application keeps working instead of failing.

this.productService.getProducts().pipe(
  catchError(error => {
    this.toastService.show('Unable to load products');
    return of([]);
  })
).subscribe(products => {
  this.products = products;
});

of([]) creates an Observable that emits an empty array. So the component still receives valid data, and doesn't have to deal with undefined.

Cleanup

finalize()

finalize() is an RxJS operator that runs when an Observable finishes — whether it completes, fails, or is unsubscribed. It is commonly used for cleanup tasks, like hiding a loader after an API call.

Yes — finalize() runs even if the API returns an error. That's exactly why it's the reliable place for cleanup.

this.loader.show();

this.http.get('/api/users').pipe(
  finalize(() => this.loader.hide())
).subscribe();
  • Hides a loader / spinner.
  • Resets UI state.
  • Stops a progress bar.
  • Cleans up resources.
Caching

shareReplay() & caching

In RxJS, caching means storing the API response so multiple subscribers can use the same data without calling the API again. The most common way to do this is shareReplay() — it stores the response and shares it with every subscriber.

private products$ = this.http.get<Product[]>('/api/products').pipe(
  shareReplay(1)
);

getProducts() {
  return this.products$; // the same cached response for every subscriber
}
Typical uses: master data, user profiles, and application configuration — anything fetched once and read from many places.

Other caching approaches also exist: a BehaviorSubject or ReplaySubject, a plain HTTP service variable, browser storage (localStorage / sessionStorage), or a state-management layer like NgRx or Signals. But for caching an API response, shareReplay(1) is the standard RxJS solution.

26

Multicasting patterns

Execution model

Unicast vs. Multicast

Unicast — every subscriber gets its own separate execution. One Observable, multiple subscribers, and each one gets a separate data stream. Angular's HttpClient observables are unicast by default — that's why the API can get called multiple times when several components subscribe to it directly.

const observable = new Observable(observer => {
  console.log('API Called');
  observer.next(Math.random());
});

observable.subscribe(data => console.log('User 1', data));
observable.subscribe(data => console.log('User 2', data));
// "API Called" logs twice — each subscribe() triggers its own run

Multicast — all subscribers share the same execution and the same data. One Observable, one execution, shared among multiple subscribers. In Angular, we usually get this with a Subject or BehaviorSubject in a service — the API is called once, and the data is shared everywhere.

const subject = new Subject();
subject.subscribe(data => console.log('User 1', data));
subject.subscribe(data => console.log('User 2', data));
subject.next('Hello');
// both subscribers log 'Hello' from the same single emission
Example: multiple components need the logged-in user's data. Instead of calling the API in every component, we use a BehaviorSubject in a service — so the API is called once, and the data is shared everywhere.
Timing model

Hot vs. Cold Observable

A cold Observable starts only when it is subscribed to, and each subscriber gets its own separate execution — this is how HTTP calls behave in Angular. A hot Observable starts even without a subscription, and it shares the same data among all subscribers — like DOM events or WebSocket streams.

Part G · Angular Signals
Angular Signals

The newer, simpler way to manage state.

27

Angular reactive features

Angular 16+

Signals

Signals are a reactive state-management feature introduced in Angular 16. They automatically track changes and update the UI whenever a signal's value changes.

  • Used for local UI state, and they work synchronously.
  • Automatically update the template when the value changes — we don't need to subscribe or unsubscribe.
  • Improve performance by reducing unnecessary change detection.
  • Good for things like a sidebar toggle, theme change, or cart count.

This is the short version. The full Signals toolkit — effect(), computed(), Signal Inputs, toSignal(), toObservable(), linkedSignal(), resource(), httpResource, and model() — is covered in Signals in depth below.

Template feature

Async pipe

Async pipe automatically subscribes to an Observable and updates the UI, and also unsubscribes automatically to avoid memory leaks. It works with both Observables and Promises.

Without async pipe — manual subscribe, need to unsubscribe, risk of memory leaks:

ngOnInit() {
  this.userService.getUsers().subscribe(data => {
    this.users = data;
  });
}
<div *ngFor="let user of users">{{ user.name }}</div>

With async pipe — no subscribe, no unsubscribe, clean code:

users$ = this.userService.getUsers();
<div *ngFor="let user of users$ | async">{{ user.name }}</div>
  • Auto subscription — when the component loads, it starts listening.
  • Auto unsubscription — when the component is destroyed, it unsubscribes. This prevents memory leaks and unwanted API calls.
  • Works with both Observables and Promises.
  • Triggers change detection, even with OnPush — it marks the component for checking when new data arrives.
  • Always shows the latest emitted value.
  • Works well with Signals-based architecture and OnPush, for performant, reactive components without manual subscriptions.
State management

NgRx

NgRx is a library used in Angular to manage application state in a centralized way. It stores all app data in one place.

  • Components don't manage data directly.
  • They just read the data and send actions.
28

Signals in depth

The rest of the Signals API — the parts that show up once you go past a simple sidebar toggle or theme switch.

Angular 16+

Why were Signals introduced?

Signals were introduced to improve Angular's performance and provide fine-grained reactivity. They reduce unnecessary change detection, simplify state management, and automatically update only the affected parts of the UI — not the whole component.

Side effects

effect()

effect() is a function that runs automatically when a signal's value changes. It is used for side effects, not for producing a value. It automatically tracks which signals it depends on, and re-runs whenever those signals change.

  • Common uses: API calls, saving to localStorage, console.log, updating the DOM.
count = signal(0);

constructor() {
  effect(() => {
    console.log('Count changed:', this.count());
  });
}
Derived state

computed()

computed() is used to create a derived signal. It automatically recalculates its value whenever the signals it depends on change.

  • Used to derive values such as a full name, a total price, or filtered data.
firstName = signal('Ram');
lastName = signal('Kumar');

fullName = computed(() => `${this.firstName()} ${this.lastName()}`);
Angular 17+

Signal Inputs

Signal Inputs are a new way for a component to receive input values as Signals, using the input() function instead of the @Input() decorator. Since the value is reactive, we can use computed() and effect() directly, without ngOnChanges() — less boilerplate, better performance. 

// Optional input
name = input<string>();

// Required input
name = input.required<string>();
Signals vs. RxJS

Are Signals a replacement for RxJS?

No — they solve different problems. Signals are mainly used for local UI state management, like a sidebar toggle, a loader state, or a selected tab, because they update the UI automatically and improve performance. RxJS is still used for handling async operations like API calls, events, and complex reactive logic.

Observable → Signal

toSignal()

toSignal() converts an RxJS Observable into a Signal. It automatically keeps the Signal updated whenever the Observable emits a new value — so we can keep using Observable-based services like HttpClient, but read the result like a normal signal.

  • No manual subscribe().
  • No async pipe needed.
  • UI updates automatically.
export class UserComponent {
  users = toSignal(this.userService.getUsers(), { initialValue: [] });
}
@for (user of users(); track user.id) {
  <p>{{ user.name }}</p>
}
Signal → Observable

toObservable()

toObservable() converts a Signal into an RxJS Observable. Use it when state is stored in a Signal, but you need RxJS operators such as debounceTime() or switchMap().

Example: a search box stores its text in a Signal. To wait 500ms (debounceTime), ignore duplicate values (distinctUntilChanged), and call the API (switchMap), first convert the Signal into an Observable.
import { toObservable } from '@angular/core/rxjs-interop';

searchText = signal('');
searchText$ = toObservable(this.searchText);

this.searchText$.pipe(
  debounceTime(500),
  distinctUntilChanged(),
  switchMap(text => this.productService.search(text))
).subscribe();
Read + write

linkedSignal()

linkedSignal() is a writable Signal whose value is linked to another Signal. It's writable, so we can update it manually, while it still recalculates automatically when the source Signal changes. Best for editable derived state.

Example: a warehouse list where the first warehouse is selected by default, the user can pick a different one, and if a new list arrives from the API, the default selection updates on its own.
warehouses = signal(['Nagpur', 'Pune', 'Mumbai']);
selectedWarehouse = linkedSignal(() => this.warehouses()[0]);

selectedWarehouse.set('Mumbai');
Async data

resource()

resource() is used for handling asynchronous data, such as an HTTP request. It automatically manages the loading, success, and error states using Signals, so we don't need to manage loading or error flags manually.

products = resource({
  loader: () => this.productService.getProducts()
});
@if (products.isLoading()) {
  <p>Loading...</p>
}
@if (products.error()) {
  <p>Something went wrong</p>
}
@if (products.value()) {
  @for (product of products.value(); track product.id) {
    <p>{{ product.name }}</p>
  }
}
Async data

httpResource

httpResource is a newer Angular feature for reactive HTTP data fetching using Signals. It automatically calls the API, manages loading and error states, and updates the UI reactively — without a manual subscribe().

Two-way binding

model()

model() is a writable Signal used for two-way data binding between a parent and a child component. It replaces the need for @Input() and @Output() with a single API.

  • Before model(): @Input() to receive data, plus @Output() + EventEmitter to send updates back.
  • With model(): one property does both jobs — no EventEmitter, no @Output(), no .emit(). 
// Parent
searchText = '';
<app-search [(search)]="searchText"></app-search>
<p>Searching for: {{ searchText }}</p>
// Child
search = model('');
search.set('Angular');
<input
  [value]="search()"
  (input)="search.set(($event.target as HTMLInputElement).value)">
Use it when: building reusable form-like components that need to both receive and update a value — a search box, dropdown, date picker, toggle switch, checkbox, or rating component.
Part H · Angular in practice
Angular in practice

The topics that come up after the basics: new syntax, routing, forms, HTTP, build tools, and architecture.

29

New template syntax & signals

Angular 17+

@if, @for & @switch

Angular 17 added control flow blocks to templates. They are faster, need no CommonModule import, and @for requires a track expression.

@if (user(); as u) {
  <p>Hello {{ u.name }}</p>
} @else if (loading()) {
  <p>Loading...</p>
} @else {
  <p>Please log in</p>
}

@for (order of orders(); track order.id; let i = $index) {
  <li>{{ i + 1 }}. {{ order.number }}</li>
} @empty {
  <li>No orders yet</li>
}

@switch (status()) {
  @case ('paid') { <span>Paid</span> }
  @case ('pending') { <span>Pending</span> }
  @default { <span>Unknown</span> }
}
  • @for gives $index, $first, $last, $even, $odd, and $count.
  • track replaces trackBy: track item.id, or track $index for simple lists.
  • Run ng generate @angular/core:control-flow to migrate old templates.
Angular 18.1+

@let declarations

@let fullName = user().first + ' ' + user().last;
@let total = price() * qty();

<h2>{{ fullName }}</h2>
<p>Total: {{ total | currency:'INR' }}</p>

The variable is read-only, and visible in the template below the declaration. It replaces the old *ngIf="x as y" trick for storing values.

Angular 14+

inject() function

@Component({ /* ... */ })
export class OrdersComponent {
  private http = inject(HttpClient);
  private route = inject(ActivatedRoute);
}

export const authGuard: CanActivateFn = () => {
  const auth = inject(AuthService);        // works in functional guards and interceptors
  return auth.isLoggedIn() || inject(Router).createUrlTree(['/login']);
};
  • inject() only works in an injection context: field initializers, constructors, factory functions, and functional guards. It does not work inside ngOnInit or subscribe callbacks.
  • Use runInInjectionContext(injector, () => ...) to call it elsewhere.
  • Benefits: less boilerplate, works with inheritance, and enables functional guards, resolvers, and interceptors.
Angular 17.3+

output(), viewChild() & contentChild()

@Component({ selector: 'app-search', /* ... */ })
export class SearchComponent {
  query = input('');                         // replaces @Input
  searched = output<string>();               // replaces @Output + EventEmitter
  box = viewChild<ElementRef>('box');        // replaces @ViewChild (a signal)
  items = viewChildren(ItemComponent);       // replaces @ViewChildren
  header = contentChild(HeaderDirective);    // replaces @ContentChild

  onEnter() {
    this.searched.emit(this.query());
    this.box()?.nativeElement.focus();
  }
}

Query signals update on their own, so you can read them inside computed() and effect() without ngAfterViewInit. See Signal Inputs and model().

Signals

effect() cleanup & untracked()

constructor() {
  effect((onCleanup) => {
    const id = this.userId();                    // tracked: the effect re-runs when this changes
    const sub = this.api.watch(id).subscribe();
    onCleanup(() => sub.unsubscribe());          // runs before the next run, and on destroy

    untracked(() => this.log.add(`Watching ${id}`));   // read or write without creating a dependency
  });
}
  • Effects run asynchronously, during change detection.
  • Writing to signals inside an effect is blocked by default. Prefer computed() or linkedSignal() for derived values; use effect() only for side effects such as logging, localStorage, or DOM work.
Angular 16+

DestroyRef

export class ChartComponent {
  private destroyRef = inject(DestroyRef);

  constructor() {
    const id = setInterval(() => this.refresh(), 5000);
    this.destroyRef.onDestroy(() => clearInterval(id));    // runs when the component is destroyed
  }
}

takeUntilDestroyed() uses DestroyRef internally. See takeUntilDestroyed.

Templates

Content projection with ng-content select

<!-- card.component.html -->
<div class="card">
  <header><ng-content select="[card-title]"></ng-content></header>
  <div class="body"><ng-content></ng-content></div>
  <footer><ng-content select="[card-actions]"></ng-content></footer>
</div>

<!-- usage -->
<app-card>
  <h3 card-title>Q2 Return</h3>
  <p>Filed on 31 July</p>               <!-- goes to the default slot -->
  <button card-actions>View</button>
</app-card>

Use ngProjectAs to move an element into a slot with a different selector. Projected content is created by the parent, even when the child decides where to show it.

Templates

ng-template & ngTemplateOutlet

<ng-template #rowTpl let-order let-i="index">
  <tr><td>{{ i + 1 }}</td><td>{{ order.number }}</td></tr>
</ng-template>

<table>
  @for (o of orders(); track o.id; let i = $index) {
    <ng-container *ngTemplateOutlet="rowTpl; context: { $implicit: o, index: i }"></ng-container>
  }
</table>

A common use is a reusable table or list where the parent supplies the cell template, passed in with @ContentChild(TemplateRef) or contentChild(TemplateRef). ng-template is also what *ngIf and *ngFor expand into. See TemplateRef.

Templates

ngClass, ngStyle & class/style bindings

<div [class.active]="isActive">...</div>
<div [style.width.px]="width" [style.color]="color">...</div>
<div [ngClass]="{ active: isActive, 'has-error': hasError }">...</div>
<div [ngStyle]="{ 'font-size.px': size }">...</div>
<div [class]="'card ' + extra">...</div>
  • Single class or style: [class.x] and [style.x]. Simple and fast.
  • Several at once or from an object: ngClass and ngStyle.
  • Binding [class] replaces only the classes it manages, in recent Angular versions.
Angular 15+

Directive composition (hostDirectives)

@Component({
  selector: 'app-button',
  hostDirectives: [
    { directive: TooltipDirective, inputs: ['tooltip'] },
    HighlightDirective
  ],
  template: `<ng-content />`
})
export class ButtonComponent {}

Every <app-button> now behaves as if it had both directives, and you choose which of their inputs and outputs are exposed. It replaces many cases of inheritance.

Pipes

Writing a custom pipe

@Pipe({ name: 'rupees', standalone: true })
export class RupeesPipe implements PipeTransform {
  transform(value: number | null, decimals = 2): string {
    if (value == null) return '-';
    return '₹' + value.toLocaleString('en-IN', { minimumFractionDigits: decimals });
  }
}
<p>{{ amount | rupees }}</p>
<p>{{ amount | rupees:0 }}</p>

Built-in pipes: date, currency, number, percent, uppercase, lowercase, titlecase, json, slice, async, keyvalue, i18nPlural. Prefer a pipe over a method call in the template: a pure pipe runs only when its input changes, while a method runs on every change detection. See Pure vs. Impure Pipe.

30

Change detection & errors

Common error

ExpressionChangedAfterItHasBeenChecked

NG0100 happens in development mode: Angular checks every binding twice, and a value was different the second time. It usually means a child changed something the parent had already rendered.

// causes the error: the parent value changes after the parent was rendered
ngAfterViewInit() {
  this.title = 'Loaded';      // the parent template already used the old value
}

Fixes:

  • Move the change earlier (constructor or ngOnInit), or compute the value instead of setting it later.
  • Use signals, which are designed to avoid this.
  • Last resort: Promise.resolve().then(() => ...), setTimeout, or this.cd.detectChanges().
  • Never use a getter that returns a new object or random value on each call, and do not change state from a method called inside the template.
Change detection

detach, reattach & ApplicationRef.tick

constructor(private cd: ChangeDetectorRef) {
  this.cd.detach();                   // this component and its children are no longer checked
}
refreshNow() {
  this.cd.detectChanges();            // check it once, manually
}
// this.cd.reattach() puts it back
  • Use it for a heavy component that updates rarely, like a big live-data table that refreshes every few seconds.
  • ApplicationRef.tick() runs change detection for the whole app.
  • Prefer OnPush and signals first; detaching is an advanced tool.
Lifecycle

ngOnChanges & SimpleChanges

ngOnChanges(changes: SimpleChanges) {
  if (changes['userId'] && !changes['userId'].firstChange) {
    this.load(changes['userId'].currentValue);
  }
  console.log(changes['userId'].previousValue);
}
  • It runs only when the reference of an input changes, so mutating an object does not trigger it.
  • Each entry has previousValue, currentValue, and firstChange.
  • With signal inputs you use effect() or computed() instead.
Debugging

Common Angular errors

ErrorMeaning and fix
NullInjectorError: No provider for Xthe service is not provided; add providedIn: 'root' or a provider
NG0100 ExpressionChangedAfterItHasBeenCheckeda binding changed during the check; see above
NG0303 Can't bind to 'x' since it isn't a known propertythe component or module is not imported, or a typo
NG0304 'app-x' is not a known elementimport the standalone component, or the module that declares it
Cannot read properties of undefineddata has not arrived yet; use ?., @if, or async
Circular dependency in DItwo services inject each other; extract a third service
Can't resolve all parametersmissing @Injectable() or wrong constructor types
Maximum call stack size exceededusually infinite recursion, or an effect that writes to its own signal

Also: ng build fails with budget errors when a bundle is too big; see bundle budgets.

31

Routing in depth

Routing

router-outlet, routerLink & child routes

export const routes: Routes = [
  { path: 'login', component: LoginComponent },
  {
    path: 'tds',
    component: TdsLayoutComponent,          // contains its own <router-outlet>
    children: [
      { path: '', redirectTo: 'dashboard', pathMatch: 'full' },
      { path: 'dashboard', component: DashboardComponent },
      { path: 'returns/:id', component: ReturnDetailComponent }
    ]
  },
  { path: '**', component: NotFoundComponent }
];
<a routerLink="/tds/dashboard" routerLinkActive="active">Dashboard</a>
<a [routerLink]="['/tds/returns', returnId]" [queryParams]="{ tab: 'errors' }">Open</a>
<router-outlet />
  • An absolute path starts with /; without it the link is relative to the current route.
  • routerLinkActive adds a class when the link matches the current URL. Add [routerLinkActiveOptions]="{ exact: true }" for exact matching.
Routing

ActivatedRoute: snapshot vs. paramMap

// snapshot: read once. Fine if the component is destroyed and re-created on every navigation
const id = this.route.snapshot.paramMap.get('id');

// observable: needed when /returns/1 changes to /returns/2 and the SAME component instance is reused
this.route.paramMap.pipe(
  map(p => p.get('id')!),
  switchMap(id => this.api.getReturn(id))
).subscribe(r => this.return = r);

// query params and fragment
this.route.queryParamMap.subscribe(q => q.get('tab'));
  • With provideRouter(routes, withComponentInputBinding()), route params, query params, and resolver data arrive as normal @Input() or input() values, so you do not need ActivatedRoute.
  • The router unsubscribes from ActivatedRoute observables for you.
Routing

router.navigate vs. navigateByUrl

this.router.navigate(['/returns', id]);                              // commands array
this.router.navigate(['edit'], { relativeTo: this.route });         // relative to the current route
this.router.navigate(['/search'], { queryParams: { q: 'tds' }, queryParamsHandling: 'merge' });
this.router.navigateByUrl('/returns/42?tab=errors');                // absolute URL string
this.router.navigate(['/login'], { replaceUrl: true });             // do not leave a history entry

state passes data without putting it in the URL: navigate([...], { state: { from: 'dashboard' } }), read with history.state. Use the Location service for back().

Routing

Router events & loading indicator

this.router.events.pipe(
  filter((e): e is NavigationStart | NavigationEnd | NavigationCancel | NavigationError =>
    e instanceof NavigationStart || e instanceof NavigationEnd ||
    e instanceof NavigationCancel || e instanceof NavigationError),
  takeUntilDestroyed()
).subscribe(e => {
  this.loading = e instanceof NavigationStart;     // show a progress bar while a lazy route loads
});

Other events: RoutesRecognized, ResolveStart, ResolveEnd, GuardsCheckStart, Scroll. Use them for analytics page views, a global loader, or scroll handling.

Routing

Preloading strategies

provideRouter(routes, withPreloading(PreloadAllModules));

// custom: preload only routes marked in route data
@Injectable({ providedIn: 'root' })
export class SelectivePreload implements PreloadingStrategy {
  preload(route: Route, load: () => Observable<any>) {
    return route.data?.['preload'] ? load() : of(null);
  }
}
// { path: 'reports', loadComponent: ..., data: { preload: true } }

See Lazy loading. loadComponent lazily loads a single standalone component; loadChildren loads a whole route tree.

Routing

Router configuration options

provideRouter(
  routes,
  withComponentInputBinding(),                         // route data as component inputs
  withInMemoryScrolling({ scrollPositionRestoration: 'enabled', anchorScrolling: 'enabled' }),
  withHashLocation(),                                  // /#/dashboard instead of /dashboard
  withRouterConfig({ onSameUrlNavigation: 'reload' })
)
  • Hash location (/#/page) works on any static host with no server rewrite rules; the default path location needs the server to return index.html for every route.
  • Page title per route: { path: 'orders', component: X, title: 'Orders' }, or a custom TitleStrategy.
  • <base href="/"> in index.html sets the root for all relative links; change it with ng build --base-href /app/.
Routing

Functional guards with inject()

export const authGuard: CanActivateFn = (route, state) => {
  const auth = inject(AuthService);
  const router = inject(Router);
  return auth.isLoggedIn()
    ? true
    : router.createUrlTree(['/login'], { queryParams: { returnUrl: state.url } });
};

export const unsavedGuard: CanDeactivateFn<FormComponent> = (component) =>
  !component.form.dirty || confirm('Discard your changes?');

{ path: 'filing', component: FilingComponent, canActivate: [authGuard], canDeactivate: [unsavedGuard] }

Class-based guards are deprecated in favor of functions. Returning a UrlTree redirects, which is better than calling navigate() inside the guard. See Route Guards.

32

Forms in practice

Angular 14+

Typed reactive forms

form = new FormGroup({
  name: new FormControl('', { nonNullable: true, validators: [Validators.required] }),
  age: new FormControl<number | null>(null),
  tags: new FormArray<FormControl<string>>([])
});

const v = this.form.getRawValue();   // { name: string; age: number | null; tags: string[] }
this.form.controls.name.setValue('Ram');
