  font-family: 'Inter';
  src: url('/fonts/inter.woff2') format('woff2');
  font-weight: 100 900;
  font-display: swap;         /* show a fallback font first, swap when ready */
}
body { font-family: 'Inter', system-ui, sans-serif; }

Use woff2, preload the main font, self-host if possible, and subset the character set. font-display: swap avoids invisible text (FOIT), and a matching fallback with size-adjust reduces layout shift.

Visual

filter, backdrop-filter, clip-path & shadows

.disabled img { filter: grayscale(1) opacity(0.6); }
.glass { backdrop-filter: blur(10px); background: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.4); }
.avatar { clip-path: circle(50%); }
.card { box-shadow: 0 1px 2px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.1), 0 8px 24px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.08); }   /* layered shadows */
.text { text-shadow: 0 1px 2px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.3); }
.overlay { mix-blend-mode: multiply; }

box-shadow does not affect layout. filter and backdrop-filter can be expensive on large areas.

Performance

contain & content-visibility

.long-section {
  content-visibility: auto;                   /* skip layout and paint until near the viewport */
  contain-intrinsic-size: auto 600px;         /* reserve estimated height to avoid scroll jumps */
}
.widget { contain: layout paint; }            /* its inside cannot affect the outside */
.will { will-change: transform; }             /* hint only; use briefly */

See Reflow vs. repaint.

Properties

pointer-events, user-select, cursor & appearance

.disabled { pointer-events: none; opacity: 0.5; }     /* ignores clicks (keyboard can still reach it!) */
.no-copy { user-select: none; }
.clickable { cursor: pointer; }
input, button { appearance: none; }                   /* remove native styling to restyle */
:root { accent-color: teal; }                         /* colors checkboxes, radios, and sliders */
textarea { resize: vertical; }

pointer-events: none does not remove an element from the keyboard tab order; also add disabled or tabindex="-1".

Properties

Text wrapping & typography properties

.long-word { overflow-wrap: anywhere; }     /* break very long words (URLs, IDs) to avoid overflow */
.code { white-space: pre-wrap; }            /* keep line breaks and spaces, but still wrap */
.title { text-wrap: balance; }              /* even out line lengths in headings */
p { line-height: 1.6; max-width: 65ch; }    /* readable text */
.num { font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums; }   /* digits line up in columns */
a { text-decoration-thickness: 1px; text-underline-offset: 3px; }

Unitless line-height (1.6) scales with the font size of each child; a value with units is inherited as a fixed length.

Layout

vertical-align & inline gaps

img { display: block; }               /* removes the small gap under an image, caused by the text baseline */
.icon { vertical-align: middle; }     /* aligns an inline icon with text */
.nav { display: flex; }               /* avoids the whitespace gap between inline-block items */

vertical-align works on inline and table-cell elements only, not on block elements. For centering in a block, use flexbox.

Animation

3D transforms & perspective

.scene { perspective: 800px; }
.card { transform-style: preserve-3d; transition: transform 0.6s; }
.card:hover { transform: rotateY(180deg); }
.face { backface-visibility: hidden; position: absolute; inset: 0; }
.back { transform: rotateY(180deg); }
.pivot { transform-origin: top left; }

perspective goes on the parent; transform-origin changes the pivot point.

Architecture

CSS reset, critical CSS & architecture

  • Reset / normalize — remove browser differences (box-sizing: border-box, zero margins, img { max-width: 100% }).
  • Critical CSS — inline the CSS needed for the first screen in <head>, and load the rest later. Angular does this for you in production builds.
  • Methodologies — BEM, utility classes (Tailwind), CSS Modules, and CSS-in-JS. Angular's View Encapsulation already scopes component styles.
  • Design tokens — keep colors, spacing, and fonts in CSS variables, so a theme change touches one place.
  • Avoid — deep selectors (.a .b .c .d), !important, overly specific ids, and styling by tag name in global files.
Part N · Scenario-based questions
Scenario-based questions

Real situations interviewers give to developers with 3 to 5 years of experience. Say how you would find the cause, what you would change, and why.

69

Performance scenarios

Scenario

A page with a 5,000-row table is slow. What do you do?

How to answer: measure before changing anything, then reduce the work.

  • Profile with Angular DevTools and the Chrome Performance tab: is it slow to render, or slow on every change detection?
  • Render less: server-side pagination or virtual scrolling, so only visible rows exist in the DOM.
  • Use OnPush on the row component, and trackBy (track row.id) so rows are not re-created.
  • Move calculations out of the template: no method calls in bindings; use a pure pipe or computed().
  • Debounce filters and sorting, and do heavy work on the server or in a Web Worker.
  • Re-measure and compare, and say the numbers.
Scenario

The app gets slower the longer a user keeps it open. How do you find the cause?

How to answer: this is a memory leak until proven otherwise.

  • Chrome DevTools, Memory tab: take a heap snapshot, use the app (open and close a page several times), take another, and compare. Look for growing counts of components or detached DOM nodes.
  • Usual suspects in Angular: Observables never unsubscribed (valueChanges, router events, interval, a Subject), setInterval, addEventListener on window, and services holding large arrays.
  • Fix with takeUntilDestroyed(), the async pipe, and ngOnDestroy cleanup. See Avoiding memory leaks and Memory leaks in JavaScript.
  • Prevent it: a lint rule for unsubscribed subscriptions, and a code review checklist.
Scenario

The first load of the app takes 12 seconds. How do you reduce it?

  • Measure: Lighthouse and the Network tab, then source-map-explorer or the bundle analyzer to see what is big. See Checking performance.
  • Lazy load feature routes with loadComponent or loadChildren, and preload the important ones.
  • Replace heavy libraries (moment with date-fns, a full lodash with single imports), and import only what you use for better tree shaking.
  • Use @defer for heavy widgets such as charts. See @defer.
  • Serve with gzip or Brotli, cache hashed files for a year, and use a CDN.
  • Optimize images (NgOptimizedImage, WebP) and fonts. Set bundle budgets so it does not regress.
Scenario

The same API is called 5 times when a page loads. How do you fix it?

  • Open the Network tab and find which components call it. Each subscribe() on an HttpClient Observable makes a separate request (cold, unicast).
  • Fix options: cache in a service with shareReplay, keep the result in a BehaviorSubject or a signal, or load it once in a resolver.
  • Also check for the same stream used twice with the async pipe in a template (each pipe is a subscription); use @if (data$ | async; as data) once.
  • For data that changes, add an invalidation rule (refresh after a save, or a time limit).
Scenario

The search box sometimes shows the results of an older search. Why, and what is the fix?

Responses can come back in a different order than the requests were sent, so a slow earlier request overwrites a newer one.

this.search.valueChanges.pipe(
  debounceTime(300),
  distinctUntilChanged(),
  switchMap(term => this.api.search(term).pipe(catchError(() => of([]))))
).subscribe(r => this.results = r);

switchMap cancels the previous request when a new term arrives, so only the latest result is shown. Debounce reduces the number of calls. Handle errors inside the switchMap so one failure does not end the stream. See catchError placement.

Scenario

Users click Save twice and get duplicate records. How do you stop it?

  • UI: disable the button and show a loader while the request is running.
  • Code: exhaustMap ignores clicks while a request is in flight.
  • Server: the real protection. Use an idempotency key or a unique constraint, because a user can double-click, retry from a bad network, or open two tabs.
this.saveClicks$.pipe(
  exhaustMap(() => this.api.save(this.form.getRawValue()).pipe(finalize(() => this.saving.set(false))))
).subscribe();
Scenario

The browser freezes when a component renders, and the console shows NG0100 or an endless loop. What do you check?

  • A method or getter in the template that returns a new object or array each time (get items() { return this.list.filter(...) } used with @for) makes Angular think the data changed on every check.
  • A child changing a parent's value in ngAfterViewInit causes ExpressionChanged.
  • An effect() that writes to a signal it reads loops forever.
  • A setInterval or setTimeout inside the zone triggers a check each tick; run it with runOutsideAngular.

Fix: compute the value once (a computed() or pipe), keep objects stable, and use OnPush.

Scenario

Users upload Excel files with 50,000 rows and the browser hangs. What do you do?

  • Best: send the file to the backend and process it there (queue plus a status endpoint); show progress by polling or WebSocket.
  • If it must be parsed in the browser, use a Web Worker, so the UI stays responsive, and parse in chunks.
  • Validate file type and size before upload, and show upload progress.
  • Show results in a paginated or virtual table, never 50,000 DOM rows. Allow cancel with AbortController or unsubscribe.
70

Data, API & security scenarios

Scenario

A user is filling a long form and the token expires. What should happen?

  • An interceptor catches the 401, calls the refresh endpoint once (shared for parallel calls), and retries the failed request.
  • If the refresh token has also expired, redirect to login with a returnUrl, and keep the unsaved form: save a draft in sessionStorage or the server, so the user returns to where they were.
  • Also: warn before a session timeout with a countdown, and use a CanDeactivate guard for unsaved changes.
Scenario

Different roles must see different menus, pages, and buttons. How do you design it?

  • After login, get roles or permissions from the token or a /me call, and keep them in a service (signal).
  • Routes: a functional guard using route data: { roles: [...] }, plus canMatch so lazy modules are not even downloaded.
  • UI: a structural directive or @if (auth.can('returns.edit')) to hide buttons and menu items.
  • Server: always enforce permissions in the API. Hiding a button is only user experience, since anyone can call the API directly.
  • Use permissions (returns.edit) and not role names in code, so roles can change without code changes.
Scenario

The backend changes an API response and your screen breaks. How do you protect against it?

  • Keep API calls in a service and map the response to your own model in one place (an adapter), so a field rename changes one file, not twenty components.
  • Type responses (interfaces), and validate risky data at runtime (a schema library such as Zod).
  • Agree on versioned endpoints (/v2/) and deprecation notices with the backend; use a shared OpenAPI spec and generate the client.
  • Add tests with HttpTestingController for the mapping, and error monitoring for undefined access.
Scenario

A security audit found an XSS issue. How do you fix it and prevent more?

  • Find the cause: usually innerHTML, [innerHTML], or bypassSecurityTrustHtml with user or API data. Angular escapes {{ }} by default. See XSS & DomSanitizer.
  • Fix: render as text; if HTML is needed, sanitize it (DOMPurify) on the server and the client, and never bypass for untrusted data.
  • Add a Content Security Policy and HttpOnly cookies for tokens.
  • Prevent: an ESLint rule against innerHTML, a review checklist, npm audit in CI, and security tests.
Scenario

The business wants live notifications and offline support. What would you use?

  • Live data: SSE for server-to-client notifications (simple, auto-reconnect), or WebSocket for two-way. Wrap it in an RxJS stream with retry and backoff, and unsubscribe on destroy.
  • Offline: a PWA service worker (PWA) caches the app shell and some GET responses; use IndexedDB for data.
  • Offline writes: store them in a queue and replay when online fires; handle conflicts (last write wins, or ask the user).
  • Show connection status in the UI, and decide what must work offline before building.
Scenario

How would you cache API data in an Angular app?

  • Rarely changing master data (states, tax sections): shareReplay in a service, or load once at startup.
  • Per-user data that changes: a signal or store with invalidation after writes, or a time limit.
  • Generic: an interceptor that caches GET responses by URL with a timeout.
  • Browser and server: Cache-Control and ETag headers, and a service worker for static assets.
  • Always answer the hard part: when does the cache become invalid (after save, on logout, after a time limit)?
Scenario

The same build must run in dev, QA, and production with different API URLs. How?

Environment files are baked in at build time, which means a separate build per environment. A better approach for deployment pipelines is to build once and load assets/config.json before the app starts, so the container or server supplies the values. See Runtime config and environment.ts files. Never put secrets in the frontend: everything shipped to the browser is public.

Scenario

After a deployment, some users still see the old version. Why?

  • index.html was cached by the browser, CDN, or service worker, so it still points to old file names. Serve it with Cache-Control: no-cache, and cache only the hashed JS and CSS files long-term. See Deploying.
  • With a PWA, the service worker serves the old version until it updates; use SwUpdate to tell the user "A new version is available, refresh".
  • A lazy chunk of the old build may no longer exist after a deployment (a chunk load error); catch it and reload the page.
71

Architecture & design scenarios

Scenario

How do you decide between a service with signals, NgRx, and local component state?

  • Local state (a form, a toggle): signals or plain fields in the component.
  • Shared between a few components: a service with signals or a BehaviorSubject, exposed read-only. See Facade pattern.
  • Large app, many teams, complex async flows, need for time-travel debugging and strict patterns: NgRx (or its SignalStore).
  • Explain the trade-off: NgRx adds boilerplate, which is worth it only when the state is big and widely shared. Do not choose it by habit.
Scenario

Your project still uses NgModules. How would you move to standalone components?

ng generate @angular/core:standalone      # run its three modes in order: convert, remove modules, bootstrap
  • Upgrade Angular first, and keep a clean git state and passing tests.
  • Run the schematic in its steps: convert declarations to standalone, remove unneeded modules, then switch to bootstrapApplication.
  • Move route by route, and lazy load with loadComponent. Keep shared modules until their parts are converted.
  • Review the diff: imports, providers, and the testing setup (imports instead of declarations). See Standalone Components.
Scenario

You must build a form with 200 fields that changes by customer type. How?

  • Describe fields in a configuration (JSON from the API: type, label, validators, visibility rules), and render with a loop that creates a FormControl per field. See Dynamic Forms.
  • Make small reusable field components with ControlValueAccessor.
  • Split into steps or sections (lazy rendered), and use FormArray for repeating rows.
  • Use updateOn: 'blur' for heavy validators, and save drafts automatically.
  • Test the config logic separately from the UI.
Scenario

How do you design a reusable table or dropdown that many teams will use?

  • Keep it dumb: data in through inputs, events out (Smart vs. Dumb). No API calls inside it.
  • Flexible with content projection and template inputs for custom cells.
  • Typed generics (<T>), OnPush, and keyboard and screen reader support (ARIA).
  • Themeable with CSS variables, and documented with Storybook examples and tests.
  • Version it as a library in a monorepo, and avoid breaking changes.
Scenario

Would you use micro frontends for this project?

Micro frontends solve an organization problem (many teams, independent releases), and they add complexity: shared dependency versions, consistent UI, routing, and performance. Mention Module Federation. For a team of about ten, a well-structured monorepo with lazy-loaded feature areas gives most of the benefit with much less cost. State the decision criteria rather than a yes or no.

Scenario

The marketing pages in your Angular app must be found on Google. What do you do?

  • Add SSR with hydration, or prerender the static pages at build time.
  • Set the title and meta description per route (Title & Meta services), a canonical URL, a sitemap, and structured data.
  • Make sure real links (routerLink renders <a href>) and semantic HTML are used. See SEO basics.
  • Avoid window and document in server code; use isPlatformBrowser or afterNextRender.
72

JavaScript, TypeScript & CSS scenarios

Scenario

You inherit a large JavaScript project. How do you move it to TypeScript?

  • Add TypeScript with allowJs and checkJs: false, so old and new files can live together.
  • Convert leaf files first (utilities, models), then outward. Use JSDoc types for files you cannot convert yet.
  • Start with strict: false, then enable noImplicitAny, then strictNullChecks, one at a time, fixing the errors. See tsconfig strict options.
  • Avoid any; use unknown and type guards. Add the build and lint steps to CI so it does not slide back.
Scenario

Production shows "Cannot read properties of undefined". How do you handle it?

  • Use error monitoring (Sentry) with source maps to see the real file and line, the user's steps, and the API response involved.
  • Typical cause: data arrives later than the template renders it, or an API field is missing for some records. Fix with @if, optional chaining, a default value, or a correct type that includes undefined.
  • Add a unit test with the failing data, and a global ErrorHandler so errors are logged and the user sees a friendly message.
Scenario

The layout works on desktop, but breaks on phones. How do you debug and fix it?

  • Check the viewport meta tag, and use DevTools device mode on real device sizes.
  • Find the overflow: a fixed width or a long unbroken word or table; outline all elements to see which one is too wide. Fix with max-width: 100%, min-width: 0 on flex children, and overflow-wrap: anywhere.
  • Use mobile-first styles, grid auto-fit, clamp(), and let wide tables scroll inside their own container.
  • Test touch target sizes, and the on-screen keyboard.
Scenario

A dropdown is hidden behind another element, even with a huge z-index. Why?

z-index only competes inside the same stacking context. If an ancestor of the dropdown has transform, opacity below 1, position with z-index, filter, or overflow: hidden, the dropdown is trapped in it (or clipped). Fix: render overlays at the end of <body> (Angular CDK Overlay or a <dialog>), or remove the property that creates the stacking context. See z-index & stacking context.

Scenario

A callback logs "undefined" for this.name, though the method worked a minute ago. What happened?

class Timer {
  name = 'Report';
  start() { setTimeout(this.log, 100); }      // this is lost: this.name is undefined
  log() { console.log(this.name); }
}
// fixes: setTimeout(() => this.log(), 100)   or   setTimeout(this.log.bind(this), 100)   or  log = () => { ... }

A method called as obj.method() has this = obj; passed on its own (const f = obj.method) it loses it. See The this keyword and call, apply & bind.

73

Team, process & delivery scenarios

Scenario

A critical bug is reported in production on a Friday evening. What do you do?

  1. Acknowledge and confirm: reproduce it or find it in the logs, and measure who is affected.
  2. Communicate: tell your lead and the stakeholders what is known, and when the next update is.
  3. Mitigate first: roll back the release, or switch off the feature flag, before searching for the perfect fix.
  4. Fix on a branch with a test that fails first, get a quick review, and deploy with a smoke test.
  5. Follow up: a short blameless post-mortem, and a prevention step (a test, an alert, a checklist).

Show calm, ownership, and communication more than heroics.

Scenario

How do you give code review feedback, and how do you take it?

  • Giving: review the logic, tests, naming, error handling, and performance risks before style (let tools handle style). Explain the why, suggest an alternative, and ask questions instead of giving orders. Mark must-fix against optional (nit:). Praise good work.
  • Receiving: do not take it personally; ask when unclear; explain your reasoning if you disagree, with evidence, and agree on a decision; thank the reviewer.
  • Keep pull requests small and with a clear description.
Scenario

You are asked for an estimate, and later you will miss the deadline. What do you do?

  • When estimating, split the work into small tasks, include testing and review, add a buffer for unknowns, and state assumptions.
  • When you see a delay, tell the lead as soon as you know, not on the due date. Bring facts and options: reduce scope (ship the core first), add help, or move the date.
  • Ask what matters most, and agree on a new plan. Learn for next time by tracking the estimates.
Scenario

You must change old, untested code that nobody understands. How do you do it safely?

  • First understand it: read it, run it, and add logging. Ask people who know the history.
  • Write characterization tests that record what the code does today, even if it looks wrong.
  • Refactor in small steps behind those tests, and commit often. Use feature flags for risky changes.
  • Leave the code a little better each time (the "boy scout rule"). A full rewrite is risky, slow, and usually repeats old mistakes; propose it only with a clear reason and a plan.
Scenario

The requirements are unclear and the product owner is not available. What do you do?

  • Write down what you understand, and your open questions with a proposed default for each.
  • Start with the parts that are clear, and design the unclear part so it is easy to change.
  • Ask a teammate or the business analyst, and send the questions in writing so the answer is recorded.
  • Show a small prototype early, because feedback on something visible is faster than a meeting.
Part O · HR & behavioral questions
HR & behavioral questions

The non-technical round: how you talk about yourself, your work, and your choices. Prepare your own real stories, and use these as a frame.

74

About you

HR

Tell me about yourself.

Structure (about 90 seconds):

  1. Present: your current role, years of experience, and main stack. ("I am an Angular developer with 4 years of experience, working on a TDS compliance platform.")
  2. Past: two or three achievements with a result. ("I cut the load time of the dashboard from 8 to 3 seconds by lazy loading and OnPush.")
  3. Future: why this role fits you and what you want to learn.

Do not read your résumé or start with your childhood. Practice it aloud until it feels natural, and keep it relevant to the job.

HR

What are your strengths?

Choose strengths the role needs (problem solving, ownership, learning fast, clear communication), and give a short proof for each: "I debug carefully. Last quarter I traced a memory leak in our grid to an unsubscribed stream and fixed it, which stopped the slowdown complaints." Avoid long lists and empty adjectives.

HR

What is your weakness?

Pick a genuine weakness that is not essential to the job, and show progress. Example: "I used to take on too much and say yes to everything. Now I estimate my capacity, tell my lead early when I am full, and prioritize with them." Avoid fake weaknesses ("I work too hard") and serious ones (missing deadlines, poor teamwork).

HR

Why are you leaving your current company?

Be honest but positive and forward-looking: "I have learned a lot and delivered our main modules, and now I want bigger technical challenges, such as larger-scale architecture, and a team where I can grow into a senior role." Do not blame your manager, colleagues, or pay. Keep it short, and do not contradict what your references would say.

HR

Why do you want to join us?

Mention something specific you learned (the product, the customers, the tech stack, the engineering culture), link it to your experience, and say what you want to contribute. Generic answers ("good company, good growth") show no preparation. Read the company website, the job description, and recent news before the interview.

HR

Where do you see yourself in five years?

"In five years I want to be a strong senior engineer: designing features end to end, mentoring juniors, and being trusted with technical decisions. I would like to grow here as the product grows." Show ambition without sounding like you will leave soon, or like you want the interviewer's job.

75

Teamwork & situations

HR

What is the STAR method?

  • Situation: the context, in one or two sentences.
  • Task: your responsibility or goal.
  • Action: what you did, step by step (spend most of your time here; use "I", not "we").
  • Result: the outcome, with numbers if possible, and what you learned.

Prepare four or five real stories (a conflict, a failure, a tight deadline, a technical win, a time you led) that you can adapt to many questions.

HR

Tell me about a conflict with a teammate, and how you handled it.

Use STAR. A good shape: two developers disagreed about an approach, you asked to understand their reasoning, compared the options against the project needs (performance, time, maintenance), maybe tried a quick proof of concept, and agreed on a decision with your lead if needed. End with the working relationship intact and a lesson. Do not blame the other person, and do not say "I never have conflicts".

HR

Tell me about a mistake or a failure.

Pick a real mistake with limited damage (a bug released without testing an edge case, a wrong estimate). Say what happened, that you took responsibility, how you fixed it quickly, and what process you changed (a test, a checklist, an earlier conversation). Employers look for honesty and learning, not perfection.

HR

How do you handle pressure and tight deadlines?

Show a method, not just "I work late": clarify priorities with your lead, split the work into small deliverables, deal with the riskiest part first, communicate blockers early, and cut scope or ask for help when needed. Give a real example with the result, such as delivering a compliance filing feature before a statutory due date.

HR

How do you work with a difficult team member or manager?

Say you first try to understand the reason (pressure, a different goal, unclear communication), talk to them directly and privately, focus on the work and facts, and adjust your communication style (written updates for a manager who is busy). Involve your lead or HR only if it does not improve. Do not speak badly about people.

HR

How do you keep your skills up to date?

Be concrete: "I follow the Angular blog and release notes, I tried signals in a small internal tool before suggesting them to the team, and I read RxJS and TypeScript documentation. I also learn from code reviews." Mention one thing you learned recently. This question is often a check on whether you know the current Angular version.

HR

Have you led or mentored anyone?

At 3 to 5 years, interviewers look for early leadership: onboarding a new joiner, reviewing pull requests, writing a short guide, owning a module or release, running a knowledge-sharing session, or helping a junior debug. Describe what you did, how you helped them grow, and the result.

76

Salary & joining

HR

What are your salary expectations?

Research the market for the role, location, and experience, and know your current pay. Give a range that you would be happy with, based on your skills and the market, and show flexibility: "Based on my experience and the responsibilities, I expect something in the range of X to Y, and I am open to discussing the full package." If you can, ask for the company's budget first. Do not lie about your current salary.

HR

What is your notice period? Can you join earlier?

Tell the real notice period. If you are asked to join earlier, say you will talk to your current employer about a buyout or an early release, but that you also want to hand over your work properly. Companies value professional exits. Do not promise what you cannot deliver.

HR

Do you have any questions for us?

Good questions: "What does the team's tech stack and release process look like?", "What would success look like in the first three months?", "How are code reviews and testing handled?", "What are the biggest technical challenges right now?", "How does the team support learning?" Do not ask about leave or pay first, and never say "No questions".

HR

Why should we hire you?

Match the job description: "You need someone who can build and maintain complex Angular modules. I have four years in Angular and RxJS, I have optimized slow screens, and I can work with the backend team on API design. I learn fast and take ownership, so I would be productive quickly." Use specifics, not superlatives.

Reactive Handbook — simple interview notes on Angular, RxJS, JavaScript, TypeScript, HTML, and CSS.
